| EPILOGUE | I would now ask ye how ye like the play,<br>But, as it is with schoolboys, cannot say.<br>I am cruel fearful. Pray yet stay awhile,<br>And let me look upon ye. No man smile?<br>Then it goes hard, I see. He that has<br>Loved a young handsome wench, then, show his<br>face—<br>’Tis strange if none be here—and, if he will,<br>Against his conscience let him hiss and kill<br>Our market. ’Tis in vain, I see, to stay ye.<br>Have at the worst can come, then! Now, what say ye?<br>And yet mistake me not—I am not bold—<br>We have no such cause. If the tale we have totd—<br>For ’tis no other—any way content ye,<br>For to that honest purpose it was meant ye,<br>We have our end; and ye shall have ere long,<br>I dare say, many a better to prolong<br>Your old loves to us. We and all our might<br>Rest at your service. Gentlemen, good night. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Exit |

| INDEX OF FIRST LINES | OF SONNETS |
| THE | Sonnets are to be found on pp.. The numbers refer to their position in the sequence.<br><br>A woman’s face with nature’s own hand painted<br>Accuse me thus : that I have scanted all<br>Against my love shall be as I am now<br>Against that time—if ever that time come<br>Ah, wherefore with infection should he live<br>Alack, what poverty my muse brings forth<br>Alas, ’tis true, I have gone here and there<br>As a decrepit father takes delight<br>As an unperfect actor on the stage<br>As fast as thou shalt wane, so fast thou grow’st<br><br>Be wise as thou art cruel; do not press<br>Being your slave, what should I do but tend<br>Beshrew that heart that makes my heart to groan<br>Betwixt mine eye and heart a league is took<br>But be contented when that fell arrest<br>But do thy worst to steal thyself away<br>But wherefore do not you a mightier way<br><br>Canst thou, Ocruel, say I love thee not<br>Cupid laid by his brand and fell asleep<br><br>Devouring time, blunt thou the lion’s paws<br><br>Farewell—thou art too dear for my possessing<br>For shame deny that thou bear’st love to any<br>From fairest creatures we desire increase<br>From you have I been absent in the spring<br>Full many a glorious morning have I seen<br><br>How can I then return in happy plight<br>How can my muse want subject to invent<br>How careful was I when I took my way<br>How heavy do I journey on the way<br>How like a winter hath my absence been<br>How oft, when thou, my music, music play’st<br>How sweet and lovely dost thou make the shame<br><br>I grant thou wert not married to my muse<br>I never saw that youpainting need<br>If my dear love were but the child of state<br>If the dull substance of my flesh were thought<br>If there be nothing new, but that which is<br>If thou survive my well-contented day<br>If thy soul check thee that I come so near<br>In faith, I do not love thee with mine eyes<br>In loving thee thou know’st I am forsworn<br>In the old age black was not counted fair<br>Is it for fear to wet a widow’s eye<br>Is it thy will thy image should keep open<br><br>Let me confess that we two must be twain<br>Let me not to the marriage of true minds<br>Let not my love be called idolatry<br>Let those who are in favour with their stars<br>Like as the waves make towards the pebbled shore<br>Like as, to make our appetites more keen<br>Lo, as a care-full housewife runs to catch<br>Lo, in the orient when the gracious light<br>Look in thy glass, and tell the face thou viewest<br>Lord of my love, to whom in vassalage<br>Love is my sin, and thy dear virtue hate<br>Love is too young to know what conscience is I<br><br>Mine eye and heart are at a mortal war<br>Mine eye hath played the painter, and hath steeled |
| *(didascalia)* | Music to hear, why hear’st thou music sadly? |
| THE | My glass shall not persuade me I am old<br>My love is as a fever, longing still<br>My love is strengthened, though more weak in seeming<br>My mistress’ eyes are nothing like the sun<br>My tongue-tied muse in manners holds her still<br><br>No longer mourn for me when I am dead<br>No more be grieved at that which thou hast done<br>No, time, thou shalt not boast that I do change!<br>Not from the stars do I my judgement pluck<br>Not marble nor the gilded monuments<br>Not mine own fears nor the prophetic soul<br><br>O, call not me to justify the wrong<br>O, for my sake do you with fortune chide win<br>O, from what power hast thou this powerful might<br>O, how I faint when I of you do write<br>O how much more doth beauty beauteous seem<br>O, how thy worth with manners maysing<br>O, lest the world should task you to recite<br>O me, what eyes hath love put in my head<br>O never say that I was false of heart<br>O that you were yourself! But, love, you are<br>O thou my lovely boy, who in thy power<br>O truant muse, what shall be thy amends<br>Or I shall live your epitaph to make<br>Or whether doth my mind, being crowned with you<br><br>Poor soul, the centre of my sinful earth<br><br>Say that thou didst forsake me for some fault<br>Shall I compare thee to a summer’s day?<br>Sin of self-love possesseth all mine eye<br>Since brass, nor stone, nor earth, nor boundless sea<br>Since I left you mine eye is in my mind<br>So am I as the rich whose blessed key<br>So are you to my thoughts as food to life<br>So is it not with me as with that muse<br>So, now I have confessed that he is thine<br>So oft have I invoked thee for my muse<br>So shall I live supposing thou art true<br>Some glory in their birth, some in their skill<br>Some say thy fault is youth, some wantonness<br>Sweet love, renew thy force. Be it not said<br><br>Take all my loves, my love, yea, take them all<br>That god forbid, that made me first your slave<br>That thou are blamed shall not be thy defect<br>That thou hast her, it is not all my grief<br>That time of year thou mayst in me behold<br>That you were once unkind befriends me now<br>The forward violet thus did I chide<br>The little love-god lying once asleep<br>The other two, slight air and purging fire<br>Then hate me when thou wilt, if ever, now<br>Then let not winter’s ragged hand deface<br>Th’expense of spirit in a waste of shame<br>They that have power to hurt and will do none<br>Thine eyes I love, and they, as pitying me<br>Those hours that with gentle work did frame<br>Those lines that I before have writ do lie<br>Those lips that love’s own hand did make<br>Those parts of thee that the world’s eye doth view<br>Those pretty wrongs that liberty commits<br>Thou art as tyrannous so as thou art<br>Thou blind fool love, what dost thou to mine eyes<br>Thus can my love excuse the slow offence<br>Thus is his cheek the map of days outworn<br>Thy bosom is endeared with all hearts<br>Thy gift, thy tables, are within my brain<br>Thy glass will show thee how thy beauties wear<br>Tired with all these, for restful death I cry<br>’Tis better to be vile than vile esteemed<br>To me, fair friend, you never can be old<br>Two loves I have, of comfort and despair<br><br>Unthrifty loveliness, why dost thou spend<br><br>Was it the proud full sail of his great verse<br>Weary with toil I haste me to my bed<br>Were’t aught to me I bore the canopy<br>What is your substance, whereof are you made<br>What potions have I drunk of siren tears<br>What’s in the brain that ink may character<br>When forty winters shall besiege thy brow<br>When I consider every thing that grows<br>When I do count the clock that tells the time<br>When I have seen by time’s fell hand defaced<br>When, in disgrace with fortune and men’s eyes<br>When in the chronicle of wasted time<br>When most I wink, then do mine eyes best see<br>When my love swears that she is made of truth<br>When thou shalt be disposed to set me light<br>When to the sessions of sweet silent thought<br>Where art thou, muse, that thou forget’st so long<br>Whilst I alonecall upon thy aid<br>Who is it that says most which can say more<br>Who will believe my verse in time to come<br>Whoever hath her wish, thou hast thy Will<br>Why didst thou promise such a beauteous day<br>Why is my verse so barren of new pride<br><br>Your love and pity doth th‘impression fill |
